Visual witness description of PDF page 18 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two side-by-side graph diagrams illustrating vertex splitting.
Left diagram (labeled "before splitting at $u$"): Five vertices $u$ (left), $a$ (top), $b$ (upper right), $c$ (bottom), and $d$ (lower right). Vertex $u$ has four incident edges: edges to $a$ and $b$ (blue lines, each labeled $y$ near $u$), forming a shaded blue triangle $uab$ with edge $ab$; and edges to $c$ and $d$ (orange/yellow lines, each labeled $z$ near $u$), forming a shaded yellow/orange triangle $ucd$ with edge $cd$. A thin vertical line also connects $a$ and $c$.
Right diagram (labeled "each copy retains weight $q_u$"): Vertex $u$ is split into two vertices, $(u, y)$ (upper left) and $(u, z)$ (lower left). Vertex $(u, y)$ is connected to $a$ and $b$, maintaining the shaded blue triangle with edge $ab$. Vertex $(u, z)$ is connected to $c$ and $d$, maintaining the shaded yellow/orange triangle with edge $cd$. Vertices $a$ and $c$ remain connected by the thin vertical line, but no edges connect $(u, y)$ to $c$ or $(u, z)$ to $a$.
```


An old triangle survives with its original mass if all six incidences qualify and the two labels agree at each of its three corners. In the ordered triangle law, each incidence has marginal $\mu$, and each corner has the law used in <a href="#eq:corner-row-agreement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:corner-row-agreement">[eq:corner-row-agreement]</a>. A union bound, followed by averaging with the triangle weights, shows that the expected fraction of triangle mass lost is at most $$O_r\!\left(\frac{\log\ell}{\sqrt\ell}
              +\sqrt{\frac{\log\ell}{\ell}}\right)
       =O_r\!\left(\frac{\log\ell}{\sqrt\ell}\right).$$ Choose a realization retaining at least the expected lower bound. The projection to $F$ is a homomorphism injective on directed edges, and composing it with the old map to $H_b$ preserves that property. All vertex weights and inherited data remain unchanged. This proves <a href="#eq:split-retention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:split-retention">[eq:split-retention]</a> and the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="subsec:triangle-iteration"></a>
#### Iteration and completion of the adjustment lemma

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-walk-entropy-bound-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-walk-entropy-bound</summary>

<a id="eq:triangle-iteration"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:triangle-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:triangle-bound">7</a>.* Apply the deletions of Subsection <a href="#subsec:triangle-preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="subsec:triangle-preparation">3.1</a> to all levels, then Lemma <a href="#lem:triangle-splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:triangle-splitting">8</a> to each nonempty level graph. Empty level graphs need no construction. The resulting graphs have neighborhood weights at most $e^{\sqrt\ell}$ and still have homomorphisms into their original $H_b$ injective on directed edges. Combining the additive losses from deletion with <a href="#eq:split-retention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:split-retention">[eq:split-retention]</a> gives $$T_{\mathrm{new}}\ge
 \left(1-C_2\frac{\log\ell}{\sqrt\ell}\right)T
       -C_3\frac{\log\ell}{\ell}M_x,
 \label{eq:triangle-iteration}$$ where the constants depend only on $r$. If the coefficient of $T$ is negative, the inequality follows simply from $T_{\mathrm{new}}\ge0$.

<a id="eq:iterated-triangle-retention"></a>
Choose a sufficiently large constant $X\ge32$, depending only on $r$. Starting at $\ell_0$, perform the reduction while $\ell>X$, replacing $\ell$ by $\sqrt\ell$ each time. Both losses in <a href="#eq:triangle-iteration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:triangle-iteration">[eq:triangle-iteration]</a> are summable uniformly in the initial value $\ell_0$. To verify this explicitly, let $p(s)=(\log s)/\sqrt s$. This function is decreasing for sufficiently large $s$, and $$\frac{p(s^2)}{p(s)}=\frac2{\sqrt s}.$$ In reverse order, the parameters at which reductions are performed start above $X$ and are successively squared. Increasing $X$ therefore makes the sum of $C_2p(\ell)$ at most $1/2$. The sum of $C_3(\log\ell)/\ell$ is bounded by a constant depending only on $r$. Iterating <a href="#eq:triangle-iteration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:triangle-iteration">[eq:triangle-iteration]</a>, and using $\prod_j(1-a_j)\ge1-\sum_ja_j$ for these nonnegative losses, gives $$T_{\mathrm{final}}\ge\frac12T_0-O_r(M_x).
 \label{eq:iterated-triangle-retention}$$

At the final stage every neighborhood has weight at most $e^X$. Every triangle still has a middle vertex. At a middle vertex $a$, the incident triangle mass, including the factor $b$, is at most $bq_aq(N(a))^2/2$. Hence <a href="#eq:middle-edge-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:middle-edge-charge">[eq:middle-edge-charge]</a> gives $$T_{\mathrm{final}}
 \le\frac{e^X}{2}
      \sum_b b\sum_{\substack{a\in V(F_b)\\a\text{ middle}}}
                     q_aq(N(a))
 \le e^XM_x.$$ This estimate also applies if $\ell_0\le X$ and no reduction is needed. Together with <a href="#eq:iterated-triangle-retention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:iterated-triangle-retention">[eq:iterated-triangle-retention]</a>, it proves $T_0=O_r(M_x)$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-walk-entropy-bound-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-walk-entropy-bound</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:adjustment">3</a>.* Proposition <a href="#prop:triangle-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:triangle-bound">7</a> bounds the contribution from triangles all of whose vertices are good by $O_r(M_x)$. The contribution from triangles meeting a bad vertex is, by <a href="#eq:bad-triangles" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bad-triangles">[eq:bad-triangles]</a>, at most $O(\Lambda^2)\sum_iw_iy_i$. Taking $B=B(r)$ sufficiently large and then increasing the lower threshold on $\Lambda$ gives $$\sum_i x_it_i\le B\sum_i x_id_i+\Lambda^4\sum_iw_iy_i,$$ which is the dual inequality <a href="#eq:dual-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dual-target">[eq:dual-target]</a>. The separation argument in Section <a href="#sec:adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:adjustment">2</a> now supplies the finite family of operations and rates in <a href="#eq:adjustment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:adjustment">[eq:adjustment]</a>. The constants are chosen in order: first the multiplier bound $A$ and the local estimate constant $C_1$, then the reduction constants and $X$, and finally $B$ and the threshold on $\Lambda$. Every choice depends only on $r$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:rounds"></a>
### Coloring rounds with dependent entry weights

We now use Lemma <a href="#lem:adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:adjustment">3</a> to extend a partial correspondence coloring while controlling the weights of the entries that remain available. Entropy and edge energy, together with compensated weight changes, are features of Johansson’s coloring method; see <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[BansalGuptaGuruganesh2018](#ref-BansalGuptaGuruganesh2018), Sections 2 and 6–8]. Here the adjustment operations act on the graph of list entries. Entries in a single list need not evolve independently; the bound on the number of entries from each part in an operation will supply the concentration needed for a simultaneous update.

<a id="eq:round-constants"></a>
<a id="eq:round-parameters"></a>
Fix $r\ge4$ and let $B=B(r)$ be the constant in Lemma <a href="#lem:adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:adjustment">3</a>. Choose $$\label{eq:round-constants}
 K=16(B+2),\qquad \gamma=0.003,\qquad C=\frac{100K}{\gamma},\qquad
 k=\left\lceil\frac{C\Delta}{\log\Delta}\right\rceil.$$ We prove Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> with $C_r=C$. Restrict each list to exactly $k$ entries, restricting the matchings accordingly, and continue to write $L(v)$ for the resulting lists. For sufficiently large $\Delta$ we have $k\le\Delta$. Throughout the coloring process, $\Delta$ denotes the maximum degree of the original graph. Set $$\label{eq:round-parameters}
 w_* = \frac Kk,\qquad \Lambda=20\log\Delta,\qquad
 \tau=\Delta^{-0.10},\qquad \xi=\Delta^{-0.14},\qquad
 \zeta=\Delta^{-0.15}.$$ The parameter $\tau$ sets the activation and adjustment rates, $\xi$ is the permitted error per round, and $\zeta$ is a smaller tolerance used in the concentration estimates. All asymptotic estimates below are uniform over the graphs, lists, matchings, and states satisfying the stated assumptions, with $r$ fixed. In particular, a factor $\Delta^{o(1)}$ in an upper bound is at most $\Delta^\varepsilon$ for every fixed $\varepsilon>0$ once $\Delta$ is sufficiently large in terms of $r$ and $\varepsilon$. The parameters $U,P_0$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:adjustment">3</a> are $\Delta^{o(1)}$, and $R_0=O(\log\log\Delta)$, at the value of $\Lambda$ in <a href="#eq:round-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-parameters">[eq:round-parameters]</a>.

Consider a proper partial correspondence coloring, and let $G'$ be the subgraph induced by its uncolored vertices. An entry at an uncolored vertex is *available* if it conflicts with none of the colors already chosen at neighboring vertices. Give each entry $i\in L(v)$, for $v\in V(G')$, a nonnegative weight $w_i$, requiring every positive-weight entry to be available. Let $H$ be the graph on the positive-weight entries whose edges are the forbidden pairs in the restricted matchings. Its parts are the sets $L(v)\cap V(H)$. The map sending an entry to its host vertex is a graph homomorphism $H\to G'$. A clique in $H$ has distinct hosts, so $H$ is $K_r$-free. The matching condition also implies that each entry has at most one neighbor in any part. These are precisely the structural assumptions of Lemma <a href="#lem:adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:adjustment">3</a>.

<a id="eq:weight-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:vertex-potentials"></a>
Use $s_i,d_i,t_i$ for the neighborhood, incident-edge, and triangle weights in that lemma, calculated in $H$. We maintain $$\label{eq:weight-bounds}
 \Delta^{-3}\le w_i\le\Delta^{-0.99},\qquad s_i\le\Lambda
 \quad (i\in V(H)).$$ For $v\in V(G')$ and $vu\in E(G')$, define $$\label{eq:vertex-potentials}
 \begin{aligned}
 W_v&=\sum_{i\in L(v)}w_i,\\
 D_{vu}&=\sum_{\substack{i\in L(v),\ l\in L(u)\\il\in E(H)}}w_iw_l,
 &D_v&=\sum_{u\in N_{G'}(v)}D_{vu},\\
 F_v&=\sum_{i\in L(v)}w_*f(w_i/w_*)
       +\gamma\log\Delta\,(K-W_v)+D_v.
 \end{aligned}$$ The entropy sum includes zero-weight entries, using $f(0)=1$. Both that sum and $D_v$ are nonnegative, and $D_v=\sum_{i\in L(v)\cap V(H)}d_i\le\Lambda W_v$. Thus an upper bound on $F_v$ prevents the total weight $W_v$ from becoming too small, while coloring neighbors decreases its edge term.

<a id="eq:initial-state"></a>
Initially, color no vertices and give every entry weight $w_*$. Since $w_*=\Theta(\log\Delta/\Delta)$ and $s_i\le\Delta w_*\le(K/C)\log\Delta$, the bounds <a href="#eq:weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-bounds">[eq:weight-bounds]</a> hold. Moreover, $$\label{eq:initial-state}
 W_v=K,\qquad F_v=D_v\le \frac{K^2\Delta}{k}
                         \le\frac{K^2}{C}\log\Delta.$$ The following proposition is the step we shall iterate.

<a id="stmt-prop-one-round"></a>
<div id="prop:one-round" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:mass-window"></a>
<a id="eq:round-conclusions"></a>
**Proposition 10** (One coloring round). *For all sufficiently large $\Delta$ depending only on $r$, suppose that a proper partial correspondence coloring and entry weights satisfy availability, <a href="#eq:weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-bounds">[eq:weight-bounds]</a>, and $$\label{eq:mass-window}
 K/2\le W_v\le2K\qquad(v\in V(G')).$$ There is an extension of the partial coloring and a choice of weights on the remaining entries that preserve availability and <a href="#eq:weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-bounds">[eq:weight-bounds]</a>, and satisfy $$\label{eq:round-conclusions}
 W_v^{\mathrm{new}}\le W_v+\xi,\qquad
 F_v^{\mathrm{new}}\le F_v+\xi,\qquad
 \frac{\deg_{\mathrm{new}}(v)}{\Delta}
 \le(1-\tau)\frac{\deg_{G'}(v)}{\Delta}+\xi$$ for every vertex $v$ that remains uncolored.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The random update and its expectation

Assume the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:one-round" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:one-round">10</a>; the case $V(G')=\varnothing$ is immediate. Fix the operations and rates $a_j$ given by Lemma <a href="#lem:adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:adjustment">3</a> on $H$, with listed supports $J_j$. We use two families of mutually independent Bernoulli flags. For each entry $z\in V(H)$ an *activation flag* is set with probability $\tau w_z$; it touches every neighbor of $z$ in $H$, with designated multiplier zero there. For each operation $j$ its flag is set with probability $\tau a_j$ and touches $J_j$. A multiplier operation has its prescribed multipliers $m_i^{(j)}$ on $J_j$, and a drop has designated multiplier zero on its singleton support. The probabilities are valid for large $\Delta$: the nonempty support and rate bound of Lemma <a href="#lem:adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:adjustment">3</a> give $a_j\le\Lambda^4$.

<a id="eq:no-flag-multiplier"></a>
For each $i\in V(H)$ put $\bar w_i=w_iY_i$. If exactly one set flag touches $i$, let $Y_i$ be its designated multiplier; if at least two set flags touch $i$, let $Y_i=0$. If no set flag touches $i$, put $$\label{eq:no-flag-multiplier}
 Y_i=1+\tau\left(s_i+\sum_{j\in\mathcal M}a_j(1-m_i^{(j)})\right),$$ where $\mathcal M$ indexes the multiplier operations and their multipliers are extended by $1$ off the listed supports. Zero-weight entries stay at zero. The coefficient of $\tau$ in <a href="#eq:no-flag-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:no-flag-multiplier">[eq:no-flag-multiplier]</a> has absolute value at most $\Lambda+\Lambda^4(1+U)$. The no-flag multiplier is therefore positive and is $1+O(\tau\Delta^{o(1)})$.

An entry *activates* when its activation flag is set. An activating entry is *successful* if none of its neighbors in $H$ activates. At every host with a successful entry, choose one such entry as its color, using any fixed order on that list to break ties. Successful entries cannot be adjacent in $H$, and they were available before the round, so this extends the proper partial coloring. Every entry conflicting with a newly chosen color is touched by its activation flag and hence has provisional weight zero, whether or not other flags also touch it. Consequently, after newly colored vertices are removed, all remaining positive provisional weights are available. The set of hosts receiving a color depends only on activation flags, independently of the choices among successful entries.

Until that removal, a bar on any quantity in <a href="#eq:vertex-potentials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vertex-potentials">[eq:vertex-potentials]</a> means that it is calculated with the provisional weights on the entire start-of-round graph $G'$. We may continue to use $E(H)$ in these sums because zero weights remain zero. The compensation in <a href="#eq:no-flag-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:no-flag-multiplier">[eq:no-flag-multiplier]</a> cancels the first-order mean loss from activations and multiplier operations. The following estimate shows how the adjustment lemma controls the remaining cost in the potential.

<a id="stmt-lem-round-expectation"></a>
<div id="lem:round-expectation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:round-expectations"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Expected changes). *For the random update just defined, $$\label{eq:round-expectations}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\bar W_v&\le W_v+o(\xi),\\
 \mathbb E\bar F_v&\le F_v+\tau(B+1)D_v+o(\xi),\\
 \mathbb E\bar D_{vu}&\ge\tfrac12D_{vu}\qquad(vu\in E(G')).
 \end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-round-expectation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-round-expectation</summary>

<a id="eq:multiplier-moments"></a>
*Proof.* Let $a_i^-$ be the sum of the rates of drops at $i$. Expanding according to the set flags touching one or two entries gives the following identities, with $il\in E(H)$ in the last line: $$\label{eq:multiplier-moments}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E(Y_i-1)
   &=-\tau a_i^-+O(\tau^2\Delta^{o(1)}),\\
 \mathbb Ef(Y_i)
   &=\tau\left(s_i+a_i^-+
           \sum_{j\in\mathcal M}a_jf(m_i^{(j)})\right)
       +O(\tau^2\Delta^{o(1)}),\\
 \mathbb E[(Y_i-1)(Y_l-1)]
   &=\tau\sum_{z\in N_H(i)\cap N_H(l)}w_z\\
   &\quad+\tau\sum_{j\in\mathcal M}
          a_j(m_i^{(j)}-1)(m_l^{(j)}-1)
       +O(\tau^2\Delta^{o(1)}).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here is a justification of the errors and the first-order terms. The sum of the probabilities of flags touching either of two fixed entries is at most $2\tau(\Lambda+\Lambda^4)$. Independence bounds both the probability of two or more such flags and the total error in replacing the exact single-flag probabilities by their marginal probabilities by $O(\tau^2\Delta^{o(1)})$. All multipliers and their $f$-values are $\Delta^{o(1)}$, so these errors remain of the stated order. For the first line, the single flags contribute $-s_i-a_i^-+\sum_{j\in\mathcal M}a_j(m_i^{(j)}-1)$ to the coefficient of $\tau$; <a href="#eq:no-flag-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:no-flag-multiplier">[eq:no-flag-multiplier]</a> cancels all but $-a_i^-$. For the second line, use $f(0)=1$ and $f(1+h)=O(h^2)$ at the no-flag value. For the third line, only flags touching both entries contribute to first order. These are common-neighbor activations and shared multiplier flags. A singleton drop cannot touch both endpoints, and a flag touching only one endpoint has an additional factor $O(\tau\Delta^{o(1)})$ from the other endpoint.

<a id="eq:entry-expected-change"></a>
For every $i\in V(H)$, including when $Y_i=0$, the entropy identity is <a id="eq:entropy-change"></a>$$\label{eq:entropy-change}
 w_*\bigl[f(w_iY_i/w_*)-f(w_i/w_*)\bigr]
   =w_i\log(w_i/w_*)(Y_i-1)+w_if(Y_i).$$ Allocate the change in $F_v$ to its positive-weight entries. The contribution of $i\in L(v)\cap V(H)$ is <a id="eq:entry-potential-change"></a>$$\label{eq:entry-potential-change}
 w_i\bigl[(\log(w_i/w_*)-\gamma\log\Delta)(Y_i-1)+f(Y_i)\bigr]
 +\sum_{l\in N_H(i)}w_iw_l(Y_iY_l-1).$$ Its expectation is at most $$\label{eq:entry-expected-change}
 \tau\left(d_i+t_i+\sum_j a_jg_i^{(j)}\right)
 +O\bigl((w_i+d_i)\tau^2\Delta^{o(1)}\bigr).$$ Indeed, <a href="#eq:multiplier-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:multiplier-moments">[eq:multiplier-moments]</a> gives the activation terms $d_i$ from entropy and $t_i$ from edge correlations. For a multiplier operation, the compensated linear terms cancel, leaving exactly $w_if(m_i^{(j)})$ and the quadratic edge terms that define $g_i^{(j)}$. A drop at $i$ contributes $w_i(1-\log(w_i/w_*)+\gamma\log\Delta)\le\Lambda w_i$ to the non-edge part and $-d_i$ to the edge part. A drop at a neighbor $l$ contributes $-w_iw_l$. These are bounded by the drop vector in Lemma <a href="#lem:adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:adjustment">3</a>. Finally, $|\log(w_i/w_*)|=O(\log\Delta)$ by <a href="#eq:weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-bounds">[eq:weight-bounds]</a>; absorbing these logarithms gives the error in <a href="#eq:entry-expected-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entry-expected-change">[eq:entry-expected-change]</a>.

Sum <a href="#eq:entry-expected-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entry-expected-change">[eq:entry-expected-change]</a> over $L(v)\cap V(H)$ and use $t_i+\sum_j a_jg_i^{(j)}\le Bd_i$. Since $D_v\le\Lambda W_v$ and $W_v\le2K$, the total error is $O((W_v+D_v)\tau^2\Delta^{o(1)})=o(\xi)$. This proves the potential estimate. The first line of <a href="#eq:multiplier-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:multiplier-moments">[eq:multiplier-moments]</a> proves the mass estimate. For each edge $il$ in the sum defining $D_{vu}$, the probability that neither endpoint is touched by a set flag is $1-O(\tau\Delta^{o(1)})$; on this event, $Y_iY_l=1+O(\tau\Delta^{o(1)})$. Nonnegativity on the other events therefore gives $\mathbb E(w_iw_lY_iY_l)\ge w_iw_l/2$ for large $\Delta$. Summing proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Concentration with bounded input participation

We next make the expectation bounds simultaneous at all vertices. The relevant concentration principle is a bounded-variable form of the generalized Hölder inequality of Finner <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Finner](#ref-Finner)] and the read-$k$ concentration method of Gavinsky, Lovett, Saks, and Srinivasan <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[GavinskyReadK](#ref-GavinskyReadK)]. We give the short proof, including the exponential-moment estimate of Hoeffding <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Hoeffding1963](#ref-Hoeffding1963)].

<a id="stmt-lem-read-concentration"></a>
<div id="lem:read-concentration" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:input-concentration"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Bounded input participation). *Let $X_1,\ldots,X_m$ be bounded real functions of finitely many independent inputs. Suppose that $X_j$ has range of length at most $b_j$, and that each input is used by at most $p$ of the functions, where $p\ge1$ is real. If $\sum_jb_j^2>0$, then for every $a>0$, $$\label{eq:input-concentration}
 \mathbb P\left(\sum_j(X_j-\mathbb EX_j)\ge a\right),\quad
 \mathbb P\left(\sum_j(X_j-\mathbb EX_j)\le-a\right)
 \ \le\ \exp\left(-\frac{2a^2}{p\sum_jb_j^2}\right).$$ If $\sum_jb_j^2=0$, both probabilities are zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-read-concentration"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-read-concentration</summary>

<a id="eq:input-holder"></a>
*Proof.* For nonnegative bounded functions $Z_j$ with the same dependence sets, we first prove $$\label{eq:input-holder}
 \mathbb E\prod_j Z_j\le\prod_j(\mathbb EZ_j^p)^{1/p}.$$ Induct on the number of independent inputs. Condition on all inputs except the last, and let $I$ index the functions using that last input. Hölder’s inequality with exponent $p$ for the $|I|\le p$ factors, and with a constant factor $1$ of reciprocal exponent $1-|I|/p$ when $|I|<p$, bounds this conditional integral by $\prod_j\widetilde Z_j$, where $\widetilde Z_j=(\mathbb E_{\mathrm{last}}Z_j^p)^{1/p}$ for $j\in I$ and $\widetilde Z_j=Z_j$ otherwise. Each $\widetilde Z_j$ uses only the remaining inputs of $Z_j$, so the participation bound still holds. The induction hypothesis applies and gives $\mathbb E\prod_j\widetilde Z_j\le
\prod_j(\mathbb E\widetilde Z_j^p)^{1/p}
=\prod_j(\mathbb EZ_j^p)^{1/p}$, proving <a href="#eq:input-holder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-holder">[eq:input-holder]</a>.

<a id="eq:hoeffding-moment"></a>
If a random variable $X$ has range of length $b$, then $$\label{eq:hoeffding-moment}
 \log\mathbb Ee^{s(X-\mathbb EX)}\le\frac{s^2b^2}{8}\qquad(s\in\mathbb R).$$ To see this, the second derivative of the logarithm on the left is the variance under the exponentially tilted law. Any law supported on an interval of length $b$ has variance at most $b^2/4$, by measuring squared distance from the midpoint. The logarithm and its first derivative vanish at zero, which proves <a href="#eq:hoeffding-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hoeffding-moment">[eq:hoeffding-moment]</a>. Apply <a href="#eq:input-holder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-holder">[eq:input-holder]</a> to $Z_j=e^{s(X_j-\mathbb EX_j)}$ and then <a href="#eq:hoeffding-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hoeffding-moment">[eq:hoeffding-moment]</a> with parameter $ps$. The resulting moment bound is $\exp(ps^2\sum_jb_j^2/8)$. Exponential Markov with $s=4a/(p\sum_jb_j^2)$ proves the upper tail; replacing $X_j$ by $-X_j$ proves the lower tail. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:vertex-concentration"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:read-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:read-concentration">12</a> first to $\bar W_v$ and to the non-edge part $\bar F_v-\bar D_v$. Apart from an additive constant, each is a sum of at most $k\le\Delta$ functions, one for each entry of $L(v)$, of range length at most $\Delta^{-0.99+o(1)}$. This follows from <a href="#eq:weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-bounds">[eq:weight-bounds]</a>, <a href="#eq:no-flag-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:no-flag-multiplier">[eq:no-flag-multiplier]</a>, and $U=\Delta^{o(1)}$; the logarithmic factors in the entropy term are absorbed in $\Delta^{o(1)}$. This also covers arbitrarily small positive provisional weights: with $0\log0=0$, $\sup_{0\le z\le b}|z\log z|=b\log(1/b)$ for $0<b<e^{-1}$. A multiplier flag occurs in at most $P_0$ of these functions, by its support bound in a single part. A drop flag occurs in at most one, and an activation flag occurs in at most one because its activating entry has at most one neighbor in $L(v)$. Thus $p=P_0$ is valid. In particular, each requirement $$\label{eq:vertex-concentration}
 \bar W_v\le\mathbb E\bar W_v+\zeta,\qquad
 \bar F_v-\bar D_v\le\mathbb E(\bar F_v-\bar D_v)+\zeta$$ fails with probability at most $\exp(-\Delta^{0.5})$ for large $\Delta$: the exponent in <a href="#eq:input-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-concentration">[eq:input-concentration]</a> is at least $\Delta^{0.68-o(1)}$.

<a id="eq:matching-concentration"></a>
For an edge $vu\in E(G')$, the matching sum $\bar D_{vu}$ has at most $k$ terms, each of range length at most $\Delta^{-1.98+o(1)}$. Every entry at either endpoint occurs in at most one term, so each flag occurs in at most $2P_0$ terms. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:read-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:read-concentration">12</a> with $p=2P_0$ shows that $$\label{eq:matching-concentration}
 |\bar D_{vu}-\mathbb E\bar D_{vu}|\le\Delta^{-1.25}$$ fails with probability at most $2\exp(-\Delta^{0.4})$. Here the exponent is at least $\Delta^{0.46-o(1)}$. This separation into non-edge terms and individual matching sums is what permits the participation bound to apply; no independence among entries of a list is assumed.

For ordinary list coloring, operations can instead be chosen separately in each graph induced by one common color label. Sampling their flags independently makes contributions of distinct colors independent, so the corresponding concentration bound has $p=1$. Arbitrary edge matchings provide no such common labels; the per-part support bound replaces that independence here.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Activations that produce colors

<a id="eq:neighbor-coefficients"></a>
<a id="eq:activated-neighbor-mass"></a>
The expectation estimate allows a potential increase of order $\tau D_v$. To offset it, we must show that enough neighbors receive colors, with their contributions measured both by $D_{vu}$ and by $1/\Delta$. Fix $v\in V(G')$, and take one of the coefficient families $$\label{eq:neighbor-coefficients}
 b_u=D_{vu}\quad(u\in N_{G'}(v)),\qquad\text{or}\qquad
 b_u=1/\Delta\quad(u\in N_{G'}(v)).$$ In either case $0\le b_u\le2K\Delta^{-0.99}$: for the first family, the matching property gives $D_{vu}\le\Delta^{-0.99}W_v$. Let $A_u$ indicate that some entry at $u$ activates. These indicators are independent over the hosts $u$, and <a id="eq:host-activation-probability"></a>$$\label{eq:host-activation-probability}
 \mathbb P(A_u=1)\ge1-e^{-\tau W_u}\ge\tau K/4$$ for large $\Delta$, since $K/2\le W_u\le2K$. Lemma <a href="#lem:read-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:read-concentration">12</a> with $p=1$ and $\sum_ub_u^2\le4K^2\Delta^{-0.98}$ gives $$\label{eq:activated-neighbor-mass}
 \sum_{u\in N_{G'}(v)}b_uA_u
 \ge\frac{\tau K}{4}\sum_{u\in N_{G'}(v)}b_u-\zeta$$ except with probability at most $\exp(-\Delta^{0.5})$. The next lemma bounds the loss caused by conflicting activations.

<a id="stmt-lem-activation-collisions"></a>
<div id="lem:activation-collisions" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:collision-tail"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Collision bound). *Let $\mathcal E_v$ be the set of edges of $H$ with an endpoint hosted at a neighbor of $v$ in $G'$, and let $X_v$ count the edges of $\mathcal E_v$ whose two endpoints activate. Then $$\label{eq:collision-tail}
 \mathbb P\bigl(4K\Delta^{-0.99}X_v>\zeta\bigr)
 \le O(\Delta^3)e^{e-1-\Delta^{0.01}}+e^{-\Delta^{0.8}}.$$ For either coefficient family in <a href="#eq:neighbor-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:neighbor-coefficients">[eq:neighbor-coefficients]</a>, the sum of $b_u$ over hosts $u$ counted by $A_u$ that receive no color is at most $4K\Delta^{-0.99}X_v$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-activation-collisions"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-activation-collisions</summary>

<a id="eq:collision-expectation"></a>
*Proof.* A host with an activating entry but no successful entry contains an endpoint of an edge counted by $X_v$. Each such edge has at most two hosts and each coefficient is at most $2K\Delta^{-0.99}$, proving the final assertion. Moreover, $$\label{eq:collision-expectation}
 \mu_v:=\mathbb EX_v
 \le\tau^2\sum_{u\in N_{G'}(v)}
               \sum_{i\in L(u)\cap V(H)}w_is_i
 \le2K\Delta\tau^2\Lambda=\Delta^{0.80+o(1)}.$$

For a possible endpoint $i$ of an edge in $\mathcal E_v$, the number of its activating neighbors is a sum of independent indicators of total mean $\tau s_i\le\tau\Lambda\le1$. Its exponential moment at parameter $1$ is at most $\exp(e-1)$, so it exceeds $\Delta^{0.01}$ with probability at most $\exp(e-1-\Delta^{0.01})$. All possible endpoints are hosted within distance two of $v$ in $G'$, and there are at most $k\le\Delta$ entries at each host. A union bound over $O(\Delta^3)$ endpoints shows that, apart from the first error term in <a href="#eq:collision-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collision-tail">[eq:collision-tail]</a>, the graph formed by the edges counted by $X_v$ has maximum degree at most $\Delta^{0.01}$.

If $4K\Delta^{-0.99}X_v>\zeta$, then $X_v>\Delta^{0.84}/(4K)$. In a graph of maximum degree at most $\Delta^{0.01}$, greedy selection of disjoint edges produces a matching of size at least $X_v/(2\Delta^{0.01})$. For large $\Delta$ this is at least $m=\lceil\Delta^{0.82}\rceil$. For $e=il\in\mathcal E_v$ write $q_e=\tau^2w_iw_l$. The events that both endpoints activate are independent along any matching, because its edges have disjoint endpoints and the activation flags are independent. Consequently, <a id="eq:collision-matching-tail"></a>$$\label{eq:collision-matching-tail}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P\left(\begin{gathered}
     \text{some size-$m$ matching in $\mathcal E_v$}\\
     \text{has all endpoints activating}
   \end{gathered}\right)
 &\le\sum_{\substack{M\subseteq\mathcal E_v\\M\text{ matching},\ |M|=m}}
                      \prod_{e\in M}q_e\\
 &\le\frac{(\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_v}q_e)^m}{m!}
   =\frac{\mu_v^m}{m!}
   \le\left(\frac{e\mu_v}{m}\right)^m
   \le e^{-\Delta^{0.8}}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The second inequality follows by expanding the power: each matching appears $m!$ times, and all other terms are nonnegative. The final inequality uses <a href="#eq:collision-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collision-expectation">[eq:collision-expectation]</a> and $m=\Delta^{0.82+o(1)}$; it is immediate if $\mu_v=0$. Combining the two exceptional events proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:colored-neighbor-mass"></a>
<a id="eq:round-failure-probability"></a>
Together, <a href="#eq:activated-neighbor-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:activated-neighbor-mass">[eq:activated-neighbor-mass]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:activation-collisions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:activation-collisions">13</a> imply that, for either choice in <a href="#eq:neighbor-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:neighbor-coefficients">[eq:neighbor-coefficients]</a>, we may require $$\label{eq:colored-neighbor-mass}
 \sum_{\substack{u\in N_{G'}(v)\\u\text{ receives a color}}}b_u
 \ge\frac{\tau K}{4}\sum_{u\in N_{G'}(v)}b_u-2\zeta.$$ Let $E_v$ be the event that <a href="#eq:vertex-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vertex-concentration">[eq:vertex-concentration]</a>, <a href="#eq:matching-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matching-concentration">[eq:matching-concentration]</a> for an edge incident with $v$, or <a href="#eq:colored-neighbor-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:colored-neighbor-mass">[eq:colored-neighbor-mass]</a> for one of the two coefficient families fails. A union bound gives, for all sufficiently large $\Delta$, $$\label{eq:round-failure-probability}
 \mathbb P(E_v)\le\exp(-\Delta^{0.005}).$$

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Simultaneous bounds and restoration of the weight conditions

<a id="eq:round-dependency-degree"></a>
We use the Lovász local lemma <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[ErdosLovasz1975](#ref-ErdosLovasz1975)] to avoid every $E_v$. Assign each multiplier flag to the host of a chosen center of a radius-$R_0$ ball in $H$ containing its listed support. Assign activation and drop flags to the hosts of the activating and dropped entries, respectively. Every input determining $E_v$ is assigned within distance $R_0+3$ of $v$ in $G'$. Indeed, the host projection does not increase distances; the provisional weights on an incident matching use flags touching entries at its two hosts; and whether a neighbor of $v$ receives a color is determined by activation flags at distance at most two from $v$. Events at distance greater than $2(R_0+3)$ therefore involve disjoint sets of independent inputs. A bound for the maximum degree of a dependency graph is $$\label{eq:round-dependency-degree}
 d=(\Delta+1)^{2R_0+10}
   \le\exp\bigl(O(\log\Delta\log\log\Delta)\bigr).$$

For completeness, the symmetric local lemma says that finitely many events of probability at most $q$, with a dependency graph of maximum degree at most $d\ge1$, can all be avoided if $q\le1/(e(d+1))$. Put $x=1/(d+1)$, so $q\le x(1-x)^d$. Induction on the number of conditioned events shows that any event, conditioned on the avoidance of any set of the others, has probability at most $x$. To make the induction step, split the conditioning set into nonneighbors and at most $d$ neighbors of the event. Independence bounds the numerator after conditioning on the nonneighbors by $q$, and the induction hypothesis bounds the probability of avoiding the neighbors, under that conditioning, below by $(1-x)^d$. The same induction ensures that all conditioning probabilities are positive. Multiplying these positive conditional avoidance probabilities proves joint avoidance. By <a href="#eq:round-failure-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-failure-probability">[eq:round-failure-probability]</a> and <a href="#eq:round-dependency-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-dependency-degree">[eq:round-dependency-degree]</a>, its hypothesis holds here for large $\Delta$.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-activation-collisions-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-activation-collisions</summary>

<a id="eq:provisional-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:potential-removal-gain"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:one-round" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:one-round">10</a>.* Fix a trial avoiding all $E_v$. Lemma <a href="#lem:round-expectation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:round-expectation">11</a>, <a href="#eq:vertex-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vertex-concentration">[eq:vertex-concentration]</a>, and <a href="#eq:matching-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matching-concentration">[eq:matching-concentration]</a> summed over the edges incident with $v$ give $$\label{eq:provisional-bounds}
 \bar W_v\le W_v+o(\xi),\qquad
 \bar F_v\le F_v+\tau(B+1)D_v+o(\xi),$$ because $\zeta+\Delta\Delta^{-1.25}=o(\xi)$. Remove the vertices colored in this trial. At a remaining vertex $v$, only the edge part of its potential changes. Each removed neighbor $u$ contributes $\bar D_{vu}\ge D_{vu}/2-\Delta^{-1.25}$ by <a href="#eq:round-expectations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-expectations">[eq:round-expectations]</a> and <a href="#eq:matching-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matching-concentration">[eq:matching-concentration]</a>. Using <a href="#eq:colored-neighbor-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:colored-neighbor-mass">[eq:colored-neighbor-mass]</a> with $b_u=D_{vu}$, the total decrease is at least $$\label{eq:potential-removal-gain}
 \sum_{\substack{u\in N_{G'}(v)\\u\text{ receives a color}}}\bar D_{vu}
 \ge\frac{\tau K}{8}D_v-\zeta-\Delta^{-0.25}.$$ Since $K/8>B+1$, the potential after removal is at most $F_v+o(\xi)$. The same requirement with $b_u=1/\Delta$ gives the degree bound in <a href="#eq:round-conclusions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-conclusions">[eq:round-conclusions]</a>, because $K/4\ge1$ and $2\zeta=o(\xi)$. It remains to restore <a href="#eq:weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-bounds">[eq:weight-bounds]</a> without losing the mass and potential estimates.

<a id="eq:trimming-cost"></a>
To *trim* an entry means to set its weight to zero. If its current positive weight is $z$, the change in the non-edge part of its host’s potential is exactly $$\label{eq:trimming-cost}
 c(z):=z\bigl(1-\log(z/w_*)+\gamma\log\Delta\bigr).$$ First trim all entries with $z>\Delta^{-0.99}$. Their costs are nonpositive for large $\Delta$, since $\log(\Delta^{-0.99}/w_*)=0.01\log\Delta-O(\log\log\Delta)$ and $\gamma<0.01$. Next trim all positive entries with $z<\Delta^{-3}$. The function $c(z)$ is increasing on this interval for large $\Delta$ and has limit zero at zero, so each cost is at most $O(\Delta^{-3}\log\Delta)$. There are at most $k\le\Delta$ entries at a host, giving total cost $o(\xi)$ there. Both trimming operations can only decrease edge terms.

At the resulting weights, mark each positive entry $i$ with $s_i>\Lambda$, and trim all marked entries simultaneously. For a marked entry of weight $z$, <a href="#eq:trimming-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trimming-cost">[eq:trimming-cost]</a> is at most $\Lambda z$ on the restored weight interval. To account for the edge loss without double counting, let $M$ be the marked entries and fix a host $v$. In the next identity, $H,w_i,s_i,D_v$ refer to the remaining graph and weights immediately before this last trim, and $D'_v$ is the edge term after it. Then <a id="eq:simultaneous-trimming-loss"></a>$$\label{eq:simultaneous-trimming-loss}
 D_v-D'_v
 =\sum_{i\in M\cap L(v)}w_is_i
  +\sum_{i\in (V(H)\setminus M)\cap L(v)}
       w_i\sum_{l\in M\cap N_H(i)}w_l
 \ge\sum_{i\in M\cap L(v)}w_is_i.$$ Each deleted edge in $D_v$ has exactly one endpoint hosted at $v$, so it occurs in precisely one of these two sums. The first sum pays for all non-edge costs at $v$, since $w_is_i>\Lambda w_i$ for marked entries. This remains true when both ends of an edge are trimmed. This last trim does not increase any $F_v$. The unmarked entries had $s_i\le\Lambda$ before the trim, and deleting weights only reduces their neighborhood weights, so the remaining entries satisfy <a href="#eq:weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-bounds">[eq:weight-bounds]</a>.

All trims preserve availability and do not increase $W_v$. Combining their costs with <a href="#eq:provisional-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:provisional-bounds">[eq:provisional-bounds]</a> and <a href="#eq:potential-removal-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:potential-removal-gain">[eq:potential-removal-gain]</a> proves the mass and potential bounds in <a href="#eq:round-conclusions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-conclusions">[eq:round-conclusions]</a> for sufficiently large $\Delta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:completion"></a>
### Iteration and completion

We finish the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> by iterating Proposition <a href="#prop:one-round" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:one-round">10</a>. The potential keeps a fixed positive amount of weight at each remaining vertex, while the maximum remaining degree decreases to a smaller order than the number of available positive-weight entries.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-activation-collisions-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-activation-collisions</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Start from the empty partial coloring and the weights in <a href="#eq:initial-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-state">[eq:initial-state]</a>, and set <a id="eq:number-of-rounds"></a>$$\label{eq:number-of-rounds}
 T_* = \left\lceil2\tau^{-1}\log\Delta\right\rceil.$$ Perform $T_*$ rounds, stopping earlier if every vertex receives a color. We verify inductively that the mass assumption <a href="#eq:mass-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-window">[eq:mass-window]</a> remains valid. After $j\le T_*$ completed rounds, every remaining vertex satisfies <a id="eq:iteration-potentials"></a>$$\label{eq:iteration-potentials}
 W_v\le K+j\xi,\qquad
 \gamma\log\Delta\,(K-W_v)\le F_v
 \le\frac{K^2}{C}\log\Delta+j\xi.$$ The upper bounds follow from <a href="#eq:initial-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-state">[eq:initial-state]</a> and <a href="#eq:round-conclusions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-conclusions">[eq:round-conclusions]</a>; the lower bound on $F_v$ follows from the nonnegative entropy and edge terms in <a href="#eq:vertex-potentials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vertex-potentials">[eq:vertex-potentials]</a>. Since $T_*\xi=O(\Delta^{-0.04}\log\Delta)=o(1)$ and $C=100K/\gamma$, these inequalities give <a id="eq:iteration-mass-window"></a>$$\label{eq:iteration-mass-window}
 K-\frac K{100}-\frac{T_*\xi}{\gamma\log\Delta}
 \le W_v\le K+T_*\xi.$$ For sufficiently large $\Delta$, both bounds lie inside $[K/2,2K]$. Availability and <a href="#eq:weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-bounds">[eq:weight-bounds]</a> are preserved by the proposition, so each successive round is justified.

<a id="eq:final-degree"></a>
<a id="eq:available-entry-count"></a>
If vertices remain after $T_*$ rounds, repeated use of the degree inequality in <a href="#eq:round-conclusions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-conclusions">[eq:round-conclusions]</a> gives $$\label{eq:final-degree}
 \frac{\deg_{\mathrm{remaining}}(v)}{\Delta}
 \le(1-\tau)^{T_*}+\xi\sum_{j=0}^{T_*-1}(1-\tau)^j
 \le\Delta^{-2}+\frac{\xi}{\tau}
 =\Delta^{-2}+\Delta^{-0.04}.$$ On the other hand, the mass lower bound and the upper bound on each positive entry imply $$\label{eq:available-entry-count}
 \bigl|\{i\in L(v):w_i>0\}\bigr|
 \ge\frac K2\Delta^{0.99}.$$ For large $\Delta$ this exceeds the remaining maximum degree, which is at most $\Delta^{-1}+\Delta^{0.96}$ by <a href="#eq:final-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-degree">[eq:final-degree]</a>. Color the remaining vertices in any order, choosing at each step a positive-weight entry compatible with the colors already chosen. Every such entry is available with respect to the partial coloring from the rounds. Each neighbor colored during this final procedure forbids at most one entry, by the matching condition. Inequality <a href="#eq:available-entry-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:available-entry-count">[eq:available-entry-count]</a> therefore guarantees a choice at every step.

The resulting coloring chooses one entry from each restricted list and avoids every forbidden pair, so it is also a coloring for the original lists and matchings. All lower thresholds on $\Delta$ in the proof depend only on $r$. Choosing a single $\Delta_r\ge3$ above them, together with $C_r=C$ from <a href="#eq:round-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:round-constants">[eq:round-constants]</a>, proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A local view of the splitting construction. All displayed incidences are assumed to qualify, and labels at vertices other than $u$ are suppressed. These are auxiliary incidence labels, not colors in the correspondence assignment. The incidences $ua, ub$ have label $y$, while $uc, ud$ have label $z ≠ y$. The triangles $uab$ and $ucd$ remain triangles after splitting at $u$; $uac$ does not. Each old edge is used once, and each copy of $u$ keeps its original weight. The coupling makes unequal labels at a triangle corner rare in weighted average.",
      "figure_id": "fig:vertex-splitting",
      "pdf_page": 18,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:vertex-splitting",
          "exact_quote": "A local view of the splitting construction. All displayed\nincidences are assumed to qualify, and labels at vertices other than $u$\nare suppressed. These are auxiliary incidence labels, not colors in the\ncorrespondence assignment. The incide",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.1cm,y=1.1cm,\n vertex/.style={circle,fill=black,inner sep=1.6pt},\n blueedge/.style={draw=blue!65!black,line width=0.9pt},\n orangeedge/.style={draw=orange!85!black,line width=0.9pt},\n every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\node[vertex,label=left:$u$] (u) at (0,0) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=above:$a$] (a) at (1.5,1.35) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$b$] (b) at (2.9,0.75) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=below:$c$] (c) at (1.5,-1.35) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$d$] (d) at (2.9,-0.75) {};\n \\draw[blueedge] (u)--node[above left]{$y$}(a)--(b);\n \\draw[blueedge] (u)--node[pos=.32,above]{$y$}(b);\n \\draw[orangeedge] (u)--node[below left]{$z$}(c)--(d);\n \\draw[orangeedge] (u)--node[pos=.32,below]{$z$}(d);\n \\draw[gray,line width=0.6pt] (a)--(c);\n \\node at (1.45,-2.05) {before splitting at $u$};\n \\draw[-{Stealth[length=2mm]},thick] (3.5,0)--(4.3,0);\n \\node[vertex,label=left:{$(u,y)$}] (uy) at (5.2,0.85) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=left:{$(u,z)$}] (uz) at (5.2,-0.85) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=above:$a$] (aa) at (6.7,1.35) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$b$] (bb) at (8.1,0.75) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=below:$c$] (cc) at (6.7,-1.35) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$d$] (dd) at (8.1,-0.75) {};\n \\draw[blueedge] (uy)--(aa)--(bb)--(uy);\n \\draw[orangeedge] (uz)--(cc)--(dd)--(uz);\n \\draw[gray,line width=0.6pt] (aa)--(cc);\n \\node at (6.5,-2.05) {each copy retains weight $q_u$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two side-by-side graph diagrams illustrating vertex splitting.\nLeft diagram (labeled \"before splitting at $u$\"): Five vertices $u$ (left), $a$ (top), $b$ (upper right), $c$ (bottom), and $d$ (lower right). Vertex $u$ has four incident edges: edges to $a$ and $b$ (blue lines, each labeled $y$ near $u$), forming a shaded blue triangle $uab$ with edge $ab$; and edges to $c$ and $d$ (orange/yellow lines, each labeled $z$ near $u$), forming a shaded yellow/orange triangle $ucd$ with edge $cd$. A thin vertical line also connects $a$ and $c$.\nRight diagram (labeled \"each copy retains weight $q_u$\"): Vertex $u$ is split into two vertices, $(u, y)$ (upper left) and $(u, z)$ (lower left). Vertex $(u, y)$ is connected to $a$ and $b$, maintaining the shaded blue triangle with edge $ab$. Vertex $(u, z)$ is connected to $c$ and $d$, maintaining the shaded yellow/orange triangle with edge $cd$. Vertices $a$ and $c$ remain connected by the thin vertical line, but no edges connect $(u, y)$ to $c$ or $(u, z)$ to $a$."
    }
  ],
